#6924. Flashpoint Receives Patent for Ransomware Risk Model, Tying Vulnerabilites to Likelihood of Attack

Cybersecurity company Flashpoint announced it has received U.S. Patent No. 12,705,360 for its Ransomware Risk model, a methodology designed to help security teams determine which newly disclosed vulnerabilities more closely resemble those historically used in ransomware attacks.

The patent comes at a time when security teams are confronting a growing vulnerability problem: Flashpoint's Global Threat Intelligence Report: 2026 Midyear Edition tracked 21,667 vulnerability disclosures during the first half of 2026, an 8% increase.

Of those, 4,015, or around 19 percent, already had public or functional exploit code. Flashpoint also reported a 45 percent increase in Ransomware-as-a-Service (RaaS) attacks during the period.

The patent was granted August 11 2026, although the underlying Ransomware Risk model isn't new. It has been available through Flashpoint Vulnerability Intelligence since 2022.

The model addresses a basic vulnerability-management problem: severity and real-world ransomware risk aren't necessarily the same thing, because severity scores do not necessarily indicate which flaws attackers are most likely to target.

The Common Vulnerability Scoring System, or CVSS, measures how severe a vulnerability could be. The Exploit Prediction Scoring System, or EPSS, estimates the probability of exploitation more broadly.

Flashpoint's Ransomware Risk model adds another dimension by comparing newly disclosed vulnerabilities with the characteristics of flaws previously exploited in ransomware attacks.

That could change where a vulnerability lands in an organization's patching queue. A lower-severity flaw might normally sit behind numerous critical vulnerabilities. But if its characteristics closely resemble vulnerabilities repeatedly targeted by ransomware operators, security teams may have reason to move it higher.

"A severity score alone can't tell you which vulnerabilities pose the greatest real-world risk of exploitation by ransomware actors," Flashpoint CEO Josh Lefkowitz said. "Our patented Ransomware Risk model applies years of threat intelligence to that problem, giving customers an earlier signal and helping them prioritize based on how attackers actually operate."

The firm's midyear research also illustrates the scale of the prioritization problem. Its vulnerability intelligence covers traditional IT systems as well as cloud, IoT, operational technology, open source software. and third-party libraries.

The company says it also tracks more than 105,000 vulnerabilities that aren't represented in CVE or the National Vulnerability Database, expanding the pool of flaws that security teams may need to evaluate.

The cybersecurity company's patented methodology profiles vulnerabilities using more than 60 technical characteristics, including whether a flaw can be exploited remotely without authentication, whether it affects operational technology systems, and how exploitation could affect data availability.

Those characteristics form a multi-factor fingerprint. The model maps each vulnerability against clusters containing flaws previously exploited in ransomware attacks and assigns a Low, Medium, High, or Critical Ransomware Risk rating.

Flashpoint says the rating can be generated when a vulnerability is disclosed, rather than requiring security teams to wait for public evidence of active exploitation. Ratings are then updated as new ransomware activity and threat intelligence emerge.

Within Flashpoint Ignite, Ransomware Risk appears alongside CVSS, EPSS, Social Risk, and exploit maturity. The idea isn't to replace existing scoring systems, but to give administrators additional information when deciding what to remediate first.

Nor does a High or Critical Ransomware Risk rating mean a vulnerability will necessarily be exploited. The model measures how closely its characteristics resemble vulnerabilities historically associated with ransomware attacks.

For security teams facing thousands of potential vulnerabilities, that distinction matters. The flaw carrying the highest severity score isn't necessarily the one that most closely resembles what ransomware operators have historically chosen to attack.

"Despite being the most popular indicator for priority, we know that severity alone doesn't tell you whether a vulnerability will be exploited or used in ransomware," said Ben Haynes, Data Science & Analytics Lead at Flashpoint and co-inventor of the patented methodology.

"A vulnerability can score low on a traditional severity scale and still share the characteristics we repeatedly see in ransomware attacks."

For more information, go to the Flashpoint site.

#6922. Before & After: How an Artist and Architect Couple Turned the “Worst House” on the Block Into Their Dream Home

In Brooklyn, artist Katherine Keltner and architect Douglas Gauthier transform a crumbling townhouse with equal parts drama and restraint.

Before they stepped foot inside the crumbling brownstone on a landmark block in Brooklyn’s Boerum Hill, artist Katherine Keltner and architect Douglas Gauthier had toured 30 homes and browsed more than 200 online. Neither of them expected to become instantly captivated by the 1869 Italianate townhouse, which was being sold in a stately yet dilapidated condition, having once served as a single-room occupancy (SRO) residence. But it happened nonetheless.

“This was the first one that had the bones that Katherine wanted,” recalls Gauthier, who runs the firm GAuthier Architects. “It follows the classic real estate rule of buying the worst house on the best block.”

The strategy paid off: Once their offer was accepted, the couple embarked on an 18-month-long odyssey that saw them jump through a series of hoops (preservationists; concerned neighbors; skeptical engineers) to completely transform the moldering pile into a creative haven for them to live, work, and raise their daughter.

A true joint-effort, Gauthier oversaw a modest two-story rear expansion and structural alterations, while Keltner took charge of spatial arrangements as well as color, pattern, texture, and artwork—much of it her own.

Once deteriorating, the interiors now have an easy elegance, with plaster walls, arched passageways, and curved corners that give the soaring ceilings an intimate feel. Pink takes an unexpected starring role, appearing throughout the parlor floor and also in the zellige-clad primary bath, but other adventurous hues like mint green and crimson also feature prominently. Capping it off is a plywood-sheathed office for Gauthier’s practice, allowing the family retreat to double as a place of creative inspiration.

Below, Keltner and Gauthier share their biggest lessons (and some “before” pictures) from the painstaking passion project.

When the townhouse was converted to an SRO, it had been chopped up into at least 13 units and two kitchens, and few of the original decorative elements were still intact. A couple had bought it in the 1960s and made it a family home again and added an art gallery space. But by 2021, when Keltner and Gauthier first saw it, the house was in a state of severe neglect, with peeling paint, crumbling floors, ancient appliances—not to mention a 1,500-pound printing press in the studio (that the couple later preserved). Yet, Keltner and Gauthier saw an opportunity, not to recreate the past, but rather forge their own set of rules.

“The house wasn’t burdened with pristine historic details—our moldings were trashed,” says Gauthier. “There were a few things that we kept and celebrated, but to me, it was like a tabula rasa. We can just go in and make it ours.” Katherine agreed: “I feel like sometimes having to preserve things is more expensive than just starting clean,” she says. “And so I thought that was an advantage for us.”

Not having to be too precious with ornamentation allowed the couple to make a bold statement about crown moldings, eschewing tradition and installing curving corners and coved ceilings instead. Nowhere does the effect have more impact than in the parlor, where Gauthier devised a ceiling light in the shape of a giant oval that casts light upward rather than down. “I really like indirect lighting,” he says.

Crafted out of laser-cut plywood, the elliptical fixture is an abstracted homage to the ceiling medallions found in neighboring historic brownstones. “Some of them are incredibly ornate and beautiful, but they don't really have any use—they’re just decoration,” says Gauthier. “Adding ambient lighting to the living space takes that historical reference in pocket.”

Once they were through the process of changing the structure’s zoning from an SRO to a single-family home, Gauthier and Keltner had to contend with its landmarked status, meaning a series of approvals were required for any additions or exterior modifications.

The couple originally planned to construct a three-story extension in the back, but they ended up compromising on two floors. This expansion includes a bump-out for the parlor floor’s kitchen, leading to a rear deck that steps down to the garden. On top, a private terrace connects to the primary bath. “Instead of having that third story, we now have a deck off of our primary suite,” says Keltner. “It’s kind of amazing,” adds Gauthier.

One decision the couple wrestled with mightily was whether to replace the original, 150-year-old pine flooring with ebony-stained white oak. “The floors were part of the reason we liked the house so much,” says Gauthier. But their contractor talked them out of keeping them. “It’s a lot of extra work for them,” says Keltner. “They have to take it up, save it, put something else down, and then put it back. I think we reached a point in time and money where we were like, Okay, fine.”

Another flooring regret was not adding underfoot heating. “I was pushing the mechanical engineers to do radiant heating and radiant cooling,” says Gauthier. “Radiant heat is really lovely—you have warmth under your feet all the time.” But the mechanics behind the system proved complicated and technically redundant, as the home was already configured with a forced-air HVAC system.

“In doing research and other projects, it’s clear that the cost-to-pleasure ratio would have been worth it,” he says now. “We regret not putting the warm floors in.”

While windows might sometimes be an afterthought, Keltner and Gauthier have advice: Go with triple-glazed. “Windows provide heat and cool insulation as well as security,” explains Keltner. “And they can define both the outside and the inside of a space.” The couple went with triple-glazed windows from Austrian-based firm Bewiso that come pre-wired for easy connection to alarm systems. “They’re almost like hurricane windows,” she says.

“You can't break through three panes of glass,” says Gauthier. Due to the building’s landmark status, they had to use historic-style windows on the front facade. “We were able to get these old-fashioned type of windows that when you close them, they feel like you're closing a Mercedes,” says Gauthier. “They completely silence the outdoors.”

The final piece of advice is perhaps the most crucial: “Hire the best contractor you can afford,” says Gauthier.

“The typical advice is to get a few bids and throw out the lowest and the highest,” says Keltner. “People usually take the second lowest.” However, that could be the determining factor between an on-time project and one that drags. “You’ll end up paying the difference both in money and time,” she says. “It’s often hard for people to bite the bullet and go with the slightly higher price, but it really pays off.”

Geoffrey Montes is an associate editor at ELLE Decor with a serious love for all things real estate and design. Before that, he worked at Architectural Digest, Galerie, and Preservation magazines, covering everything from jaw-dropping listings to world-famous architects and design events like Salone del Mobile and Homo Faber.

#6921. Experts Say This Is the Best Way to Get Rid of Fruit Flies—and You Probably Have It in Your Pantry

Every item on this page was hand-picked by a House Beautiful editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

Few things are less pleasant than finding insects taking over your kitchen. Fruit flies may not be as alarming as cockroaches or stink bugs, but they’re still a nuisance—and they’re a signal that you’re not cleaning as thoroughly as you should be.

Fruit flies often congregate in sticky, sweet, moist spots: around fresh produce, by the sink, or near the trash. With their persistent buzzing and quick breeding cycle, a few occasional flies can become a much larger problem if you don’t deal with them quickly.

Fortunately, fruit flies are pretty manageable. The first step is to find what they’re drawn to, whether it’s overripe fruit, food spills, or a trash can or compost bin that isn’t sealed properly. Once you’ve found the source, a few straightforward steps can help clear them out and keep the problem from spreading. Homemade traps and smarter food storage are among the practical ways to take back your kitchen.

For expert advice on getting rid of fruit flies, we spoke with Diana Ludwiczak, a certified home pest inspector in New York City. She explains how to deal with an infestation effectively and keep the flies from returning.

“Fruit flies are attracted to ripe and rotting fruits and vegetables, so it’s important to keep your kitchen clean and free of any food debris,” says Ludwiczak. “Wipe down counters, sweep or vacuum the floors regularly, and store fruits and vegetables in the refrigerator to reduce their exposure to the air.”

Fruit flies also like fermented items, including beer, liquor, and wine. They breed in dark, damp areas like sink drains, garbage disposals, trash cans, and mop buckets.

“Once they begin reproducing indoors, females are able to lay about 500 eggs, and the eggs will hatch in as little as 24–30 hours after being deposited by the female,” according to Orkin.

To get rid of fruit flies in your home, you can try this expert-recommended method.

“Fill a small jar or cup with apple cider vinegar and a few drops of dish soap, and then cover the container with plastic wrap tightly,” Ludwiczak suggests. Poke a few holes in the wrap and place the jar near the fruit fly infestation. “The fruit flies will be drawn to the smell and will become trapped in the jar. This is my number one tip, that I personally use every summer!” she adds.

Within a day, you should see many fruit fly carcasses floating in the liquid. You'll want to keep changing out the liquid each day until all the flies have been caught.

Getting rid of fruit flies is just the first step. Here's what Ludwiczak wants you to do to prevent them from coming back.

When you bring home fresh produce from the grocery store, wash it off before storing or displaying it.

“Fruit flies are also attracted to standing water, so it’s important to dry out sinks, showers, and other areas where water can accumulate. Make sure to fix any leaky pipes or areas of your home where water tends to accumulate,” says Ludwiczak.

According to Ludwiczak, rinsing produce with a vinegar solution may keep your produce fresher for longer—and this helps stave off a fruit fly infestation. To make it, Ludwiczak mixes around two parts water and one part vinegar and sprays it onto the produce. Make sure to give your fruits and veggies one more final rinse in water before you’re done, though, so the vinegar flavor and scent don’t stick around.

Screening in your windows is a good idea for preventing all kinds of critters from getting in, but they're also effective against fruit flies.

“Make sure to use screens on windows and doors to prevent fruit flies from entering your home,” Ludwiczak says. “This is especially important during the warmer months when windows and doors are more likely to be open.”

Basic housekeeping is enough to keep fruit flies in check. Just make sure to keep your countertops clean, move overripe fruit to the fridge, take out your garbage regularly, and clean out your sink to prevent it from becoming a breeding ground for bugs.

#6919. SailPoint Intros New Capabilities to Address Agentic AI Identity Security Challenge

AI agents are moving from experiments into everyday enterprise environments, but the security controls around them are not keeping pace, according to enterprise identity security company SailPoint. In its latest Horizons of Identity Security report, SailPoint found that 79% of enterprises already have AI agents running in production, while just 2% have deployed identity security tools specifically designed to govern them.

That gap could become harder to ignore as organizations give agents more autonomy. Agents can increasingly access applications, sensitive data, and credentials, and in some cases take actions on a user's behalf, effectively creating a new class of identity that IT and security teams need to manage.

SailPoint, which develops identity security and access governance technology for enterprises, is responding with a collection of new identity security capabilities announced at its Navigate 2026 conference. The company is expanding its Agentic Fabric to discover "shadow AI" agents and provide runtime authorization and controls capable of immediately disabling an agent.

The company also introduced what it calls SailPoint Autonomous Agents, specialized AI agents designed to govern other agents. The security agents can monitor runtime activity, intercept threats, and adjust permissions while allowing legitimate agents to continue operating, SailPoint said.

The idea reflects one of the challenges created by increasingly autonomous AI systems: Traditional human approval processes may not be fast enough to govern software capable of taking large numbers of actions on its own.

"The idea that a security admin is going to review and approve access for a group of autonomous agents making 10,000 tool calls a second isn't just outdated — it's a fantasy," SailPoint CTO Chandra Gnanasambandam said.

Another part of SailPoint's approach focuses on visibility.

New endpoint and browser sensors, along with SIEM and XDR telemetry and non-human identity discovery capabilities, are designed to uncover agents, MCP servers, credentials, tools, and data stores that haven't been brought under an organization's identity program.

The company is also introducing Autonomous Identity Security Posture Management, or A-ISPM, which uses identity information from its Atlas platform to identify dormant accounts, orphaned access, shadow administrators, and other potentially risky access paths. Organizations can then revoke access, disable an identity or AI agent, assign an owner or automate enforcement.

Just-in-Time provisioning is another part of the strategy. Rather than giving people, service accounts, and AI agents permanent access, permissions can be granted when needed and subjected to conditions such as reauthentication or a business justification.

Capabilities across SailPoint's Agentic Fabric, Human Fabric, and Atlas platforms are beginning to roll out globally. A-ISPM and several other announced features are expected in the company's fourth quarter of fiscal 2027.

"Autonomous agents are no longer just experimental copilots; they are becoming the primary execution layer of modern enterprises," SailPoint President Matt Mills said.

For more information, go to the SailPoint site.

#6916. Would You Give the Shirt Off Your Back for a Vintage Prada Skirt?

How about a Comme blazer? At a RealReal event last week, guests cast their votes—literally

Last week, The RealReal toasted its new book Real Style: Dressing With Intention & Expressing Yourself Through Style with a thought-provoking event in downtown New York. The secondhand retailer always draws a fun crowd of fashion designers, models, editors, stylists, and all-around creatives—essentially its most stylish customers. The evening’s festivities slotted into a busy week of after-work events, but this one was different. There was an unexpected twist discovered upon entry. The brand had curated racks of stellar vintage and secondhand fashion. Guests could pick any item they so pleased—but with a caveat. They had to leave one item of clothing that they were currently wearing behind.

No one was alerted beforehand because that would defeat the purpose. You were meant to think about these new items in the context of your own favorite items: this wasn’t a white elephant exchange, but a test of individual perceptions of value, desire, and, in some cases, desperation. I saw someone sneaking out in a sleeveless Prada dress over jeans and tall boots. (No judgment.) The “Take One, Leave One” experience, as described by the brand, involved “no purchases, no transactions, just the ancient ritual of exchange, reimagined as a social experiment challenging the psychology of consumerism.”

I arrived early and approached the task with hesitancy. Could I be bothered? Do I really need any of this? But the racks were really good. Old Prada. Jil Sander. Dries Van Noten. Comme des Garçons. Even Romeo Gigli. Deciding what I was prepared to give away felt like a mind game. Sure, the sparkly Rachel Comey skirt fetches a higher dollar value than my J.Crew sweater, but I wear my sweater at least once a week, my go-to white button-down every two. And no one is touching my favorite Repetto Zizis.

As I walked around, the racks began to empty, guests pulling items on over their clothes or waiting in an increasingly long line for a fitting room. This was a particularly interesting experiment in a room populated by the fashion industry—better hope you weren’t wearing too good an outfit that day. But then I saw something that changed everything: a Colleen Allen fleece jacket, Victorian-inspired, fastened with hook-and-eye closures from her fall 2024 collection. It’s been a niche fashion It item ever since—and I had fallen victim.

In the end, it did come down to simple fashion math for me: I could buy another white shirt, while I was not getting my hands on that jacket anytime soon without some form of help—or a steep discount. And as I sit here writing this in my new fleece—after it was showered with compliments in the office—I can’t stop thinking about what I would give up for my own personal grails, after I quite literally took the shirt off my back.

A friend traded her earrings for a Celine cuff bracelet. Another, her fancy socks for Dries heels—kind of cheating, but oh well. I ended up giving up something still available to be repurchased. The skirt I was wearing, however, was old enough to classify as vintage, or at least the older side of secondhand, so there was no way I was finding that one again. Its value is much harder to quantify as it’s personal. As the brand’s new book says: “Don’t buy anything. Until you know what you want.”

#6913. Survey: Most Organizations Are Not Consistently Measuring AI Value Against Costs

A new study from consulting firm KPMG found that while AI investment continues to grow, relatively few organizations consistently measure whether the technology's benefits justify its costs. The company's Global AI Pulse Q3 2026 report found that 89% of organizations report measurable value from AI, while just 12% consistently assess that value against costs across the organization.

Average planned AI investment for the next 12 months has risen to $210 million, compared with $186 million in the first quarter of 2026.

The findings are based on responses from 2,131 senior leaders across 20 countries and jurisdictions, surveyed between July 23 and August 26, 2026.

Most organizations have established ways to track AI spending: 61% review costs during project approval, and 59% monitor them once systems are operating.

Consistently connecting those costs to value remains less common. Beyond the 12% that do so across the organization, another 26% assess value against costs for most initiatives.

Organizations reporting established AI returns have stronger measurement practices. Among this group, 48% consistently assess value against costs, four times the proportion in the overall survey group.

Cost-monitoring dashboards are also more common among organizations reporting established returns: 77% use them, compared with 43% of those still experimenting.

"The organizations seeing a return did the implementation and the transformation properly: guardrails in place, workforce ready, data cleaned up," said Priya Emmanuel, global head of aIQ at KPMG International. "That is a sequence, not a secret."

Organizations are also developing more formal systems to manage AI deployment.

Fifty-five percent operate a formal AI management layer, which KPMG calls a "harness layer," comprising controls and tools between AI models and business applications.

Security and identity controls, along with data access controls, were the most common capabilities, each reported by 43% of respondents. Model routing, which determines which model handles a particular task, was reported by 23%.

Meanwhile, 53% assign accountability for AI-informed decisions to a named executive, the CEO, or an executive committee.

The proportion reporting significant employee adoption of AI agents rose from 25% in the first quarter to 34% in the third quarter.

Cybersecurity concerns were more common among organizations with mature AI programs.

Half of those reporting established AI returns identified cybersecurity as a barrier, compared with roughly one-third of those still experimenting.

Overall, 86% of respondents said their organizations are adapting their cybersecurity operating models to address AI-accelerated threats.

The report identifies accountability, operational resilience, and cost management as priorities as AI becomes more embedded in business operations.

"Many organizations have made AI models widely available and track their use," said Swaminathan Chandrasekaran, head of the US AI Center of Excellence at KPMG. "But consumption alone is not a strategy."

For the full report, visit the KPMG site.

#6912. Designers Say THIS Is the Secret to Making Framed Posters Look Like Expensive Art

Your prized prints deserve better than a thumbtack.

Posters have come a long way since your dorm-room days. A vintage travel ad, a favorite movie poster, or a concert print you’ve kept for decades can bring as much character to a room as an original painting. The key to giving them a more polished look? A thoughtful frame, creative placement, and the confidence to display what you love.

"Never overthink putting posters up in your home, especially concert posters," says interior designer J.D. Bell. "Your home should reflect your personality, interests, and what you love."

Here, designers share their favorite ways to give posters the elevated treatment they deserve.

The quickest way to transform a poster into a statement piece is to ditch the inexpensive clip frame in favor of something more substantial. Consider rich wood, sleek metal, or even an ornate antique frame that contrasts with the poster's graphic quality.

"The key to making them feel elevated is to treat them with the same consideration you would a fine piece of art," says designer Hattie Sparks Collins. "A beautiful frame, thoughtful installation, and a little juxtaposition go a long way."

For particularly colorful or busy posters, a generous mat can provide visual breathing room, while a frame that picks up one of the artwork's less prominent colors creates a subtle, sophisticated connection.

Have a stack of concert posters gathering dust? Turn them into a gallery wall that tells your story.

Bell recently displayed a collection of vibrant Dead & Company concert posters against black-and-gray Zak + Fox patterned wallpaper in a client's home. Rather than using identical frames, he selected a mix of black styles to introduce texture while maintaining cohesion.

"The darker backdrop really made the colorful artwork pop," he explains. The collection was installed in a hallway leading to the client's office, a fitting tribute to his love of music.

The takeaway? Don't be afraid to experiment with patterned walls, unexpected locations, and subtly mismatched frames.

One of the most compelling ways to display posters is alongside furnishings that feel decidedly more traditional. Think a framed vintage movie poster above an antique chest, or a bold graphic print surrounded by rich textiles and classic upholstery.

"I really love pairing graphic, colorful posters with antiques, interesting textiles, and more traditional furnishings," Collins says. "That tension between high/low and old/new is what makes a room feel collected instead of decorated."

Ultimately, the most stylish poster is one that means something to you. Forget whether it's rare, valuable, or considered fine art. A beautifully framed memento from your favorite concert can become the most interesting piece in the room.

As Bell puts it, "What matters is making those pieces feel special and intentional. The right framing can do a lot for a poster and make it feel like a real piece of art."

#6908. Report: Cyber Attacks Increasingly Exploit Trusted Workplace Tools

In its latest threat intelligence report, Ontinue found that attackers are increasingly using trusted workplace tools to trick users into giving them access to enterprise networks. Approximately 30% of organizations monitored by the cybersecurity company experienced at least one attempt to impersonate IT support through Microsoft Teams during the first half of 2026. About one in every 990 external Teams contacts showed signs of someone pretending to be an internal IT worker.

Ontinue's report says the trouble is that these attacks blend in with ordinary workday communication, which makes them harder to catch.

"The majority of effective attacks today don't rely on sophisticated exploits. They rely on trust," Balazs Greksza, director of Advanced Threat Operations at Ontinue, said in the company's announcement.

Ontinue broke down how a typical attack works: An employee gets an unexpected Teams message or call from someone claiming to work in the organization's IT department. The person warns of a security issue, a software problem or an update that can't wait. Then they walk the employee through opening Windows Quick Assist, installing remote-access software, or running a PowerShell command. Following those steps can hand the attacker control of the employee's computer, often without the employee realizing it.

The approach is very similar to the Black Basta ransomware group, which helped popularize similar attacks in 2024. Unfortunately, Ontinue found the technique has since been adopted by other cybercriminal groups, including some with no known link to Black Basta.

Some threat agents are also combining tactics. They might first overwhelm an employee's inbox with unwanted e-mails, then reach out through Teams pretending to be IT support offering to fix the problem.

"Phishing no longer resides solely in your inbox. It has spread across the entire corporate communications environment," said Mika Aalto, co-founder and CEO of Hoxhunt.

Teams impersonation is only part of the problem. Ontinue also found attackers taking advantage of legitimate Microsoft sign-in processes. Between May and June, researchers identified 10 confirmed device-code phishing compromises across five customer tenants.

In these attacks, criminals trick employees into completing what appears to be a normal Microsoft authentication process. But doing so can give the attacker access to the employee's account and system without actually breaking their password.

The report also found attackers using blockchain-based infrastructure to hide malicious activity, accounting for more than 25% of confirmed malicious ClickFix findings.

"Attackers are no longer looking for gaps in your perimeter. They are looking for the systems your organization already trusts and finding ways to move through them undetected," said Shane Barney, chief information security officer at Keeper Security.

The security firm says that IT needs to keep a closer eye on unexpected Teams contacts, remote-support requests, suspicious PowerShell activity, and changes to authentication settings. Employees should also confirm sensitive requests through established internal channels, rather than relying on the name or account that appears on their screen.

Aviv Nahum, co-founder and CEO of Above Security, noted that AI is making these attacks more convincing by helping criminals imitate familiar voices, writing styles, and conversations.

"The security model therefore must shift from 'does this look real?' to 'can I independently verify that this person is who they claim to be?'" Nahum said.

The full Ontinue report can be found here.

Chris Paoli (@ChrisPaoli5) is the associate editor for Converge360.

#6907. Plant Experts Say Right Now Is the Best Time to Buy These 13 Essentials for Next Year’s Garden

You can save some serious cash by shopping early.

If you love to dig in the dirt, it’s never too early to think about next year’s garden. While this year’s growing season is winding down, planning ahead can save you money and time next spring. Most garden centers, local nurseries, and online vendors are clearing out stock now. Plus, anything you buy now will be ready for the first sunny days next spring when you can’t wait to get outdoors again.

“Fall is the ideal time to take advantage of opportunities like helping garden centers clear out what they can’t store over winter or sell the following year,” says Stephanie Rose, author of The Wild & Free Garden and founder of Garden Therapy. “This is also true for the secondhand marketplace, as many neighbors will want to rehome garden supplies and plants, particularly perennial divisions, to avoid winter storage. Check your community buy-and-sell groups in fall to stock up for the spring garden.”

As a garden writer, my personal favorite stock-up item this time of year is garden gloves. I typically go through two to three pairs of the nitrile-coated gloves per season. Because you can toss them in the washer after use, I like to keep a few pairs on hand—so I’m always grabbing a second or third pair when I see them on sale in the fall.

Ahead, here’s what else gardening experts say to stock up on now to save money and get ready for next spring.

Spreading compost or amendments in garden beds now gives them more time to work into the soil before spring planting season, and you’ll probably find some good deals on them.

“I also like to stock up on end of season mulch so it’s there in the spring when I want it,” says Natalie Carmolli, public relations specialist and shrub expert with Proven Winners ColorChoice Shrubs. “Just be sure to cover it so it doesn’t get waterlogged in the winter.” Stash a bag or two in your shed or under tarps so it’s ready for early spring plantings without needing to make a trip to the home store.

Even if you’re careful, outdoor containers get weathered and don’t last forever.

“Look especially for decorative containers, which can get really expensive. If you know you'll need some next year, fall clearance is a great time to grab them,” says landscape designer Katherine Aul Cervoni, founder and principal of Staghorn NYC. “Pottery Barn and Rejuvenation have some of my favorites.”

And if it’s time to repot some of your houseplants that have outgrown their containers, you’ll find some deals now.

“Anything that retailers don’t want to store for the season is easy to pick up at a discount,” says Rose. “Lanterns, string lights, and pathway lights are fabulous finds for fall, given that the days are shorter and a little extra lighting will help extend your time in the garden.”

You may think it’s too late to plant, but fall actually is one of the best times to plant deciduous shrubs!

“Cooler air and warm soil let roots establish without summer heat stress, so plants come out of dormancy in spring with a head start,” says Carmolli. “The exception is evergreens: they don’t love being planted late in the season and are better off waiting until spring. Because they don’t go dormant, planting them before their roots have a chance to get fully established can hurt their survival.”

The same goes for perennials. “They get deeply discounted in fall when they are finished blooming,” says Rose. “Garden centers won’t want to spend a year tending to them, so they will often price them on clearance. Fall is the best time to plant perennials, so you can do a major garden glow-up on a budget.”

Are your pruning shears dull and don’t cut cleanly any longer? Is the handle on your trowel loose? Do you need a sturdier garden spade? Autumn is a great time to replace and upgrade tools.

“Grab them now when you’re thinking about it,” says horticulturist Justin Hancock, director of research and development and horticultural content with Costa Farms and author of The New Complete Guide to Houseplants. “Plus you’ll usually find them on clearance in the fall.”

“As long as they're stored somewhere cool and dry, most seeds will keep for at least a few years,” says Cervoni. “Heads up, though: some varieties, such as carrots and spinach, have a much shorter shelf life of one year tops.”

Think trays, heat mats, starter pots, and seed-starting mix. If you start seeds indoors in late winter to early spring, any supplies you find now will likely be cheaper than in February when they hit the shelves again, says Carmolli.

Tomato cages, trellises, stakes, and plant rings are things you don't necessarily think about until you need them, and then suddenly you're scrambling to find them.

“I personally am going to invest in a taller tomato cage for my cherry tomatoes since they always end up taller than I remember,” says Cervoni.

Hose nozzles, watering wands, sprinklers, and other fittings are a great category for purchasing in the fall.

“I feel like there's always something that needs replacing come spring and you'll be happy you already have them on hand once the warm weather arrives,” says Cervoni.

Whether you’re planning to start seeds indoors next spring, bringing houseplants indoors to overwinter, or you just want to give your year-round houseplants an extra boost of light during the dark winter months, consider buying grow lights.

“If you want to keep your houseplants growing optimally through the winter, investing in some supplemental lighting can make a big difference as days grow shorter and the light gets more intense,” says Hancock. “You don’t necessarily need fancy and expensive plant lights. Even an LED light can give your houseplants a little extra boost.”

Fall is the time to buy these anyway, but you can sometimes find great deals late in the season. Get them planted before the ground freezes. This varies by zone, but it’s typically no later than the end of the year across most zones because bulbs need time in the ground to stimulate spring blooming, says Cervoni.

What’s better than curling up with a good book and a cup of hot cocoa as the weather gets too cold to garden?

“Fall is the perfect time to buy gardening and houseplant books so you can dig in and get information and inspiration all winter long for an even more successful spring garden, keeping your green thumb alive all winter,” says Hancock.

Follow House Beautiful on Instagram and TikTok.

#6906. King Charles III Gets Into the Bahamian Spirit As He Joins Locals at a Junkanoo Parade

The monarch kicked off his nine-day Caribbean tour

King Charles III is living it up on his Caribbean tour. The British monarch is currently on his three-day state visit to the Bahamas, after which he will head to Guyana for two days and then Antigua and Barbuda—where he will attend the Commonwealth Heads of Government Meeting (CHOGM)—for four days.

Queen Camilla will meet him for the final stop, but for now, he is fully enjoying his solo adventure. Yesterday, the king joined a Junkanoo parade in Nassau’s Parliament Square and, in his crisp light-gray suit, danced the day away with locals. Photos and videos show the royal shaking a pair of cowbells in the national colors of the Bahamas while jigging along next to festively-dressed dancers.

He wore his gray suit with a white button-up, a black-and-red striped tie, caramel suede boots, orange-frame sunglasses, and a patterned pocket square. Vacation attire!

A Junkanoo parade is a vibrant street festival with dancing, music, and colorful costumes. While the celebrations usually just take place during official holidays—like Boxing Day and New Year’s Day—a special one was coordinated for Charles’s visit.

Charles’s nine-day Caribbean tour leads up to the Commonwealth Heads of Government Meeting, and therefore serves as a diplomatic mission to reinforce connections between commonwealth nations, discuss shared priorities, and get to know local communities.

The trip marks Charles’s first time in the Bahamas as king, and his first visit in 53 years (he visited the country for Bahamian Independence Day in 1973, when he was Prince Charles).

During his engagements this time around, the king has been sure to address the region’s colonial history and Britain’s involvement in the transatlantic slave trade.

#6904. This “Cool Girl” British Lighting Brand Is Sold on Amazon, and I Just Bought Its Cutest Lamp

Every item on this page was hand-picked by a House Beautiful editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

When it comes to buying home decor, I follow a strict “bougie on a budget” philosophy. Sure, I love to fill my space with charming, elevated items, but I’m not exactly willing to spend a small fortune to outfit my rental apartment. So you can imagine my sheer surprise and delight when I learned you can buy a lamp from Pooky Lighting right on Amazon.

If you know, you know, but just in case you don’t? The British brand has been the cool girl’s secret to great lighting ever since it crossed the pond in 2024. Pooky might sell every fixture imaginable—and even have collaborations with Novogratz and Morris &. Co.—but its pièce de résistance is the portable, rechargeable lights.

A whimsical glow that goes wherever you go? Sold...well, if they weren’t so expensive. A quick scroll shows prices starting at $115 and easily going above $200. Not exactly conducive to my “bougie on a budget” mindset. When I stumbled upon two Pooky portable lights for $100 on Amazon, I thought it was a mistake or a very convincing knockoff. However, upon further inspection, these lights seem legit. Though both lights have the same lower price tag, they have two completely different looks. With a pleated shade and bulbous base, the Cecily lamp will pair nicely with midcentury-inspired or even contemporary spaces. If, like me, you are looking for something more aligned with Pooky’s English roots, might I suggest the Primrose style? The woven shade and scalloped details fit perfectly with my apartment’s traditional coastal vibe. Both options come in seven colorways, so you’re bound to find one that matches your interiors.

As soon as my lamp arrived, I charged the Genesis Cell light source, which can reportedly hold a charge for up to 17 hours, and immediately placed it on my kitchen counter. I always joke I’m allergic to harsh, overhead lighting, so I love how this fixture illuminates my culinary space without making the entire room obnoxiously, blindingly bright. And, since it's cordless, I can really take it anywhere. Next to my oven when I cook? Above the dishwasher when I’m only washing plates and glassware? Near the coffee machine to make my morning brew? All of the above, and then some.

But perhaps my favorite detail is the accompanying remote control, which has a timer setting. It’s all too easy to turn on a portable lamp, forget about it, and wind up with zero battery the next time you need a light. With this fixture, I can light my kitchen for 15, 30, or 60 minutes and have it magically turn off on its own. Now that’s a bright idea.

Follow House Beautiful on Instagram and TikTok.

#6903. Inside the Lavish Estates Where The Gilded Age Was Filmed

These locations are straight out of 1880s high society.

Every item on this page was chosen by an ELLE Decor editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

Why should the Brits have all the fun? The writer of Downton Abbey, Julian Fellowes, went stateside with the HBO series The Gilded Age, which tells the story of New York City high society in the 1880s amid the halcyon days of the industrial boom. The series explores the tension between old-money elites and their escalating feud with the nouveau riche neighbors who threaten the established social order. Meanwhile in real life, powerhouse families like the Vanderbilts and the Carnegies left their opulent footprints on Newport, Rhode Island, in the form of their famously ostentatious and absolutely gargantuan “summer cottages.” Circumstances which lend themselves to set-design magic.

In honor of season four of The Gilded Age, which premiered November 1, we’re revisiting the real-life settings that have helped bring the series to life. The social alliances may shift, but the architecture remains a dependable scene-stealer. Below, tour 21 properties that rank among the East Coast’s architectural crown jewels, from historic house museums to lavish mansions.

Features as: Bertha Russell’s ballroom and George Russell’s bedroom, as well as the billiard room.

These so-called dollar princesses and their families lived in Manhattan and spent their summers in Newport, Rhode Island. Of course, these industry magnates didn’t consider their infrequent visits (some only came a few weeks in a year) as a reason to exercise restraint. The mammoth Breakers mansion is one of four so-called summer “cottages” (read: castles) that were completed just blocks from each other in a building boom between 1892 and 1902. The mansion was designed by Richard Morris Hunt in the Renaissance Revival style, as a less-than-subtle symbol of the Vanderbilt family’s social and financial preeminence in the Gilded Age. It was architectural one-upmanship that is considered the grandest of all the manses, featuring ornate ballrooms, extensive art collections, manicured gardens, and the latest (then) in modern technology, including electric lights. It has 70 rooms, an interior using marble imported from Italy and Africa, and a library mantel purchased from a château in France.

“When you walk into the dining room of The Breakers, with its painting of the goddess Aurora on the ceiling 50 feet above you or see 500,000 cubic feet of marble in a variety of different hues, you can’t help but be awestruck by the incredible wealth of these Gilded Age tycoons,” says Trudy Coxe, the chief executive officer and executive director of The Preservation Society of Newport County, which has under its stewardship six of the grand mansions featured in the series.

The preservation society worked closely with the filming crew to ensure the safety of the objects and spaces. “We had long discussions about the billiard table at The Breakers,” she explains. “It could not be removed, but it was important to the scene being filmed. Rather than moving it, we worked with their props department to put an alternative covering on the table to simulate an actual game of pool.”

In the series, the Breakers’ neo–Italian Renaissance great hall and music room are featured as Bertha Russell’s (played by Carrie Coon) ballroom, which we see in the final ball scene. George Russell (Morgan Spector) also discusses business while playing billiards in the mansion’s massive billiard room.

Features as: Bertha Russell’s bedroom in the Russell residence

Marble House was built from 1888 to 1892 as a summer cottage for Alva and William Kissam Vanderbilt. It was also designed by Richard Morris Hunt, but in the Beaux-Arts style. For an American house, it was unparalleled in opulence, with a temple-front portico resembling that of the White House, which had been completed 92 years earlier in 1800 (lofty, no?).

Mrs. Russell’s bedroom, built on a soundstage in Bethpage, New York, was inspired by real-life socialite Alva Vanderbilt’s bedroom in the Marble House. The French Louis XV bed was painted and reupholstered in salmon-pink velvet, and the custom bedding used 300 yards of fabric that have been draped, cinched, and swagged.

And that’s just the textiles. Most of the room’s furniture is refurbished antique and everything in the room has been painted to match. Sometimes, Shaw notes, it was difficult to paint a lovely wooden antique piece. “The scenic artists would look at us and say, ‘You really want me to paint over the chestnut? Are you sure?’” says Shaw. “Yes.”

Consuelo Vanderbilt’s (Alva and William’s child) monochrome red bedroom in the mansion also serves as George Russell’s bedroom in the show.

Features as: The exterior of Sylvia Chamberlain’s house

Rosecliff was built for Nevada silver heiress Theresa Fair Oelrichs, whose father, James Graham Fair, was one of the four partners in the Comstock Lode. The famed architectural firm McKim, Mead & White was commissioned to design the mansion, which was modeled after the Grand Trianon, the garden retreat of French kings at Versailles. The French Baroque Revival gem was designed for entertaining at a grand scale—and it certainly fulfilled its purpose. Fair Oelrichs would become known as one of the three great hostesses of Newport.

The white front facade of this mansion, which recently got a $7.4 million renovation, is a stand-in for Sylvia Chamberlain’s (played by Jeanne Tripplehorn) house. You’ll see it in Episode 7, when Tom Raikes (Thomas Cocquerel) pays a visit.

Features as: The servant kitchen and the real-life Sarah Berwind’s bedroom as parts of the Russell residence

The Elms is a Classical Revival dwelling that was completed in 1901. Architect Horace Trumbauer designed it for the coal baron Edward Julius Berwind, taking inspiration from the 18th-century d’Asnières French château. It also houses an impressive collection of paintings and tapestries, as well as the latest technology of its age.

In The Gilded Age Seasons 1 and 2, The Elms stands in as part of the Russells’ lavish residence, including scenes in the kitchen and Bertha Russell’s bedroom. But it truly steps into the spotlight in Season 3, when it hosts a dazzling ball. And as a long-awaited confession of love looms (we won’t spoil from whom), the opulent Newport mansion proves to be the perfect setting for high drama and high society.

Each guest is granted a cinematic entrance, illuminated by a golden glow that bathes the grand façade—a new addition this season, as electricity is now becoming available. “The house’s interiors would not have been electrified yet, but people were beginning to use it for parties,” Shaw says. “That would mean they would have to have their own generator installed somewhere on the property to light up those lights.” To accomplish this, the crew went to specialty shops that make reproduction bulbs. “The original bulbs were much bigger than what we have in a household today,” Shaw adds. “Some of the lightbulbs we used were 10 inches in diameter, which is how big they were then.”

Features as: The exterior is used for Caroline Astor’s Beechwood house; the boardinghouse is used for a scene with Oscar van Rhijn; and another room serves as Agnes van Rhijn’s bedroom

Completed in 1852, Château-sur-Mer is one of the first grand Bellevue Avenue mansions of the Gilded Age in Newport, built by architect Seth C. Bradford for William Shepard Wetmore. Its interiors were designed by Ogden Codman Jr., who co-authored Edith Wharton’s Decoration of Houses. The mansion was host to many major parties, including the Fête Champêtre, an elaborate country picnic for more than 2,000 guests in 1857, as well as a debutante ball for Edith Wetmore in 1889. Wetmore’s son, George Peabody Wetmore, went on to have a distinguished political career as the governor of Rhode Island and, later, as a U.S. senator.

In The Gilded Age, the home’s exterior was used for Caroline Astor’s (Donna Murphy) Beechwood House. The hallway, dining room, and ballroom were used during Mamie Fish’s (Ashlie Atkinson) doll tea party. Several bedrooms serve as Agnes van Rhijn’s (Christine Baranski) bedroom, Oscar van Rhijn’s (Blake Ritson) bedroom, Anne Morris’s (Katie Finneran) bedroom, and Patrick Morris’s (Michel Gill) office. There’s also a scene filmed at the boardinghouse with Oscar van Rhijn, according to Patch.

Features as: The office of Brook family lawyer Tom Raikes

Hunter House was constructed in 1748 at the harborfront near the north end of the Newport Historic District at a time when the city was a bustling seaport in the British empire. The house was owned by wealthy merchants and overlooked the wharves that serviced their trading ships. It’s one of the finest examples of Colonial architecture in Newport, according to the Preservation Society. Inside, its resident families furnished the house with decorative arts produced by expert craftsmen, much of which has been preserved today. Such a luxurious lifestyle, however, depended upon the labor of enslaved people of African descent; recent research by the Preservation Society has identified at least 13 enslaved individuals who lived and worked at Hunter House. Today’s Hunter House honors their perseverance and incalculable contributions to Newport’s past.

One of the sitting rooms in Hunter House functions as the office of Brook family lawyer Tom Raikes, and is set fictitiously in Doylestown, Pennsylvania.

Features as: The Astor family summer home

Belcourt was completed in 1894 for Oliver Hazard Perry Belmont, a prominent American socialite and coachman who served in the U.S. House of Representatives. Like many of the Newport mansions, the Château-esque structure was completed by architect Richard Morris Hunt; it was designed to mimic the hunting lodge of Versailles, built for Louis XIII of France, but with a special ask to accommodate Oliver’s collection of horses and automobiles. In 1896, Oliver gave the house to his second wife, the former Alva Vanderbilt, who designed the neighboring Newport mansion Marble House. Alva used Belcourt as a gathering place for the elite of society, hosting lavish parties and balls and attracting notable figures in politics, business, and the arts.

In The Gilded Age Season 1, Belcourt shows up in Episode 8 as Mrs. Astor’s lavish new Newport home, which Mrs. Russell sneaks inside and is forced to flee to avoid a less-than-pleasant meetup.

Features as: The social gathering scene where Caroline Astor and George Russell meet

Built in 1867 by prolific Boston tea and china merchant Robert M. Cushing, the Ledges was originally the summer home of the Cushing family. This majestic oceanfront pile is perched on one of Newport’s most desirable peninsulas, overlooking Bailey’s Beach. It was built by architect John H. Sturgis, who designed a large, Stick-style cottage that was equal parts comfort and elegance. Today it features original furnishings, papered walls, and unique artwork, including a studio that was added to the side of the house for Robert’s artistic children.

Today, the estate remains in the hands of the Cushing descendants, who occasionally open the house up to film crews. It was featured a few years ago in the film Evening, starring Glenn Close and Meryl Streep. “Working at the Ledges was pretty extraordinary,” Gilded Age location manager Lauri Pitkus told The Providence Journal, “because I don't think I’ve ever been in a home with spectacular views out of every window.”

This historic house was built in 1864 for John Noble Alsop Griswold, an Old China Trade merchant and diplomat. The house is the work of Richard Morris Hunt; it was one of the earliest buildings in the American Stick style of architecture. The house has a complex roofline with a main mansarded section that is pierced by numerous gable and dormer sections. It boasts a number of balconies sheltered by deep eaves, with gable ends decorated with woodwork. Today, the house is a gallery location for the Newport Art Museum. In the series, the house features in Season 1 Episode 1, and again in Episode 5.

Features as: Mingling with the other elite scene

The International Tennis Hall of Fame and its perfectly preserved grass courts are in the Newport Casino, a National Historic Landmark property. The historic seven-acre estate was commissioned by New York Herald publisher James Gordon Bennett as a social club for the elite summer residents of Newport. The Newport News boasted three days after it opened in July 1880: “It is doubtful if a livelier place can be found.” Today, the space is recognized as the official hall of fame for the sport of tennis globally. Since 1955, 262 distinguished inductees representing 27 countries have been enshrined in Newport.

In the eighth episode of The Gilded Age Season 1, the characters are seen promenading around the piazza that overlooks what is known today as Horseshoe Court. A few characters are even playing tennis. “It was fitting to have that history depicted here, given The Gilded Age’s look into that time period,” the hall of fame’s Megan Erbes tells ELLE DECOR. “Several of our staff members got to work closely with the production team and actors, teaching everything from the history of our property to how to hit a proper forehand.” RI Monthly reported that crews returned to the courts last summer to film Season 2 of the show, so it’s possible that Newport Casino will make another appearance.

Features as: The parlor, library, entryway, and staircase of the Morris house, located on Fifth Avenue in the show; Mr. and Mrs. Morris’s bedrooms were filmed in the Newport mansions

William Smith Slater hired General William Walker to build a home as a wedding gift for his daughter. The result was this historic home, which was completed in 1877. The Victorian house is filled with treasures—the fireplace surrounds, for example, were carved by Charles Dowler, who came to the United States to produce arms for the Civil War. Today, the house is open to visitors and is known as the Clouds Hill Museum.

The Clouds Hill Museum is the only Rhode Island site outside of Newport that was used in the series. The crew scheduled filming for Season 1 in late February of 2021, when a few hundred people were on site during two days of filming, museum director Wayne Cabral told Warwick Online, noting that the taping finished around midnight on the second day of filming (after five days of work to prep the set). Almost all the parlor’s furniture was removed, and the head set designer brought in items sourced from local antiques stores in East Greenwich and North Kingstown, museum president Anne Holst added. The producers were adamant about removing the existing curtains, which were disintegrating, Holst added, so she negotiated to leave the replacements (which cost $10,000) with the museum after filming.

Features as: Visit to the home of Aurora Fane scene

The Gothic Revival–style Lyndhurst Mansion was designed in 1838 by Alexander Jackson Davis. Lyndhurst’s third owner, Jay Gould, is the real-life inspiration for the “new money” character George Russell. “He was fantastically ruthless in business, to an extent that Mrs. Astor would never let him in, he was never allowed into society, and she didn’t let his children in until he was dead,” creator, writer, and producer Julian Fellowes said in a podcast. “But he was an incredibly affectionate father and a very caring and faithful husband…and his children all had these nostalgic memories of Lyndhurst, their house on the Hudson.”

In Season 1 of The Gilded Age, Lyndhurst’s interiors are the home of characters Charles and Aurora Fane (Ward Horton and Kelli O’Hara), who are related to the van Rhijns and part of the “old money” side of society. The Lyndhurst lawns and grounds also doubled as Central Park and the Sheep’s Meadow in the opening sequence. The home boasts a bowling alley that dates back to 1894, which was re-created to be the ferry terminal that Marian Brook (Louisa Jacobson) and Peggy Scott (Denée Benton) take to Manhattan for the first time in Episode 1. And finally, the house’s carriage house was transformed into The New York Globe newspaper offices.

The Belvedere Estate, located between Lyndhurst Mansion and writer Washington Irving’s Sunnyside home, is a century-old mansion on 25 acres in historic Tarrytown. The estate was originally part of Philipsburg Manor, a 90,000-acre property built for Florence and Casper Whitney. “While the mansion dates from the 1920s, it has a much older European feel that could represent something from decades earlier,” says Belvedere Estate sales director Linda Fiorentino, who recalls the massive legwork required to film at the mansion.

“I will never forget the background actors descending from a yellow school bus, fully dressed in period costumes and felt hats,” she says. The Season 1 scene was filmed at the pond at the back of Belvedere Estate, acting as a stand-in for Central Park in the scene when Marian meets Mr. Raikes at Bethesda Fountain in Episode 1. The pond is a man-made feature, and the water level sometimes sinks to a mere foot or two, Fiorentino explains. “Before filming, they filled the pond with roughly 600,000 gallons of water to fill to capacity, giving the illusion of a much larger body of water and to allow them to have pleasure boats filled with passengers on the water,” she says. They also re-created Central Park’s Shakespeare Bridge from rough-hewn logs and brought in trees, shrubs, and foliage to cover the existing link fence because it was too modern.

Features as: The drawing room of wealthy widow Sylvia Chamberlain

Toward the end of the 19th century, Colonel Elliott Fitch Shepard, a lawyer who founded the New York State Bar Association in 1876, purchased more than 500 acres on a hill high above the Hudson River north of Tarrytown. He engaged the renowned architecture firm McKim, Mead & White to design his family’s manor house here. The result was a 75-room Victorian structure that mimicked 18th-century Italian villa architecture. Think: hand-carved mahogany paneling, a grand stairway near the front entrance, high patterned ceilings, and elaborate cornice work. Unfortunately, Shepard died before it was completed in 1893, and it passed into the hands of William Rockefeller and Frank Vanderlip. These men were part of a group (including John Jacob Astor, who died on the Titanic in 1912) who went on to convert the building to the Sleepy Hollow Country Club, which remains a private members-only club today.

Scenes in the drawing room of wealthy widow Sylvia Chamberlain were shot on location here. “The entire room was redressed by our team in the Rococo style,” set decorator Regina Graves told 1st Dibs.

Features as: Part of Mrs. Astor’s residence

Glenview was built in 1876 by John Bond Trevor, a highly successful stockbroker, who engaged New York City architect Charles W. Clinton to build his estate. The mansion was a paragon of luxury and modern comfort, featuring indoor plumbing, gas illumination, and a coal-burning furnace to circulate heat throughout its 37 rooms. The exterior had elements of Victorian Gothic and French Second Empire design, complete with a mansard roof, a gray stone facade constructed of blocks hewn from a Hastings-on-Hudson quarry, and decorative sandstone elements brought from Indiana.

The mansion makes an appearance as Caroline Astor’s residence in Season 1. “They were all so complimentary about Glenview, and of course we did a lot of polishing and waxing,” Laura Vookles, chair of the museum’s curatorial department, told The Rivertown Enterprise. “They focused on our exquisite interiors and filmed in six rooms, including the dining, library, and sitting rooms, and used all but one painting that is normally on view.”

The John Paine Mansion, also known as the Castle, was built in 1896 and was deemed the grandest house in Troy, New York. It has since served as a private residence, a college administration building, a movie set (see: Martin Scorsese’s 1993 drama The Age of Innocence), and, currently, a fraternity house. In the show, one of the bedrooms in the Castle features as Tom Raikes’s office.

This residence, the home of Troy locals Bill and Sue Comiskey, stands in for the Brooklyn residence of the well-off Scott family. At one point early in the series, Arthur Scott (John Douglas Thompson) and his daughter, Peggy (Denée Benton), sit on carved-oak chairs around a damask-covered dining table. The home’s elaborate details really shine in this historic home, which dates back to the turn of the 19th century. Stained-glass windows and an elegant china cabinet are memorable architectural features that are original to the location and transport the viewers back in time.

While filming what would be the Scott family residence, Bill, the homeowner, told News10 that a few prized family possessions actually made the cut. “My desk was in it, front and center…you [also] saw the whole front and back parlor into the dining room where they were having a meal,” he explained. “[There were] great shots of this room catching the stained glass, the fireplace, [and] this bookcase my grandfather made.”

Features as: The interior is used as George Russell’s workspace

Hempstead House was built for Howard Gould by architects Hunt & Hunt in 1912 on the north shore of Long Island. The mansion was eventually sold to art mogul Daniel Guggenheim (yes, that Guggenheim). Hempstead House is one of four mansions on the grounds of the Guggenheim Estate—which includes Castle Gould, Falaise, and Mille Fleur. The Tudor-style castle was the embodiment of Gold Coast opulence, a place where the esteemed family hosted grand parties, performances, and exhibitions by gatherings of the elite of the time. To say it is massive would be an understatement: 50,000 square feet, with 40 rooms and a 60-foot-tall entry foyer. In The Gilded Age, the mansion’s interior was used as George Russell’s workspace.

Features as: The late General Henry Brook’s house

Rock Hall Museum was initially an 18th-century plantation. The two-and-a-half-story Georgian-style home was built in approximately 1767 and later restored to its original appearance in the 1950s. In Season 1 Episode 1, the house’s white facade is featured within the first 15 minutes as a backdrop for the late General Henry Brook’s house. Marian, his daughter, is seen walking out of the home on her way to the train station.

The Hart Cluett Museum is a work of Federal architecture designed by Martin E. Thompson in 1827. He was hired by William Howard. Thompson brought the design of the Hart Cluett House back to New York City, where many examples of the 1827 Federal-style townhouse were copied throughout Manhattan. This house, a museum open to the public today, was featured in The Gilded Age in Episode 2 as Anne Morris’s home.

Rachel Silva is a freelance writer and editor covering design, architecture, and culture. She previously served as an editor at ELLE Decor, and her work has appeared in Harper’s Bazaar, TIME, and Veranda, among others. A graduate of Columbia Journalism School, she is based in New York City.

#6902. Sophos Launches Tool for Shadow AI Defense

Unauthorized artificial intelligence tools and agents are creating security risks for organizations as AI adoption increases, according to cybersecurity company Sophos. In a recent product announcement, the company highlighted the growing use of shadow AI, referring to AI tools and agents operating without formal organizational approval or oversight. The security firm cited research indicating that 69% of organizations suspect or have evidence of unauthorized AI use.

Separate research from Gartner and Cloud Security Alliance found that 82% of organizations discovered shadow AI agents in their environments during the previous year.

According to the security firm, organizations have struggled to identify which AI tools employees use, what information those tools access, and whether their use complies with security policies.

"Organizations are adopting AI faster than they can secure it, and most security teams can't answer three basic questions: which AI tools are running, what data is going into them, and whether that activity lines up with policy," said Kyle Falkenhagen, senior vice president of product management at Sophos.

The company also cited an IDC projection that the number of AI agents could reach 1.3 billion by 2028.

Sophos researchers reported observing attackers use AI agents within a customer's network to build and test nearly 80 malware modules.

The cybersecurity firm announced the general availability of Sophos AI Defense, a security offering designed to identify AI tools, monitor their activity, and enforce organizational security policies.

The product operates through Sophos Fusion, the company's cybersecurity platform, and uses information from devices, networks, identity systems, and browsers.

According to the company, AI Defense can inspect prompts, uploads, and other AI interactions to identify potentially risky activity and help prevent sensitive data exposure.

For more information, go to the Sophos site.

Enterprise AI projects are running into infrastructure questions as organizations move from planning toward deployment, according to recent research from Digital Realty.

OpenAI has announced it will retire Custom GPTs and move the specialized workflows built around them to a new plugin architecture spanning ChatGPT and its Codex coding agent.

As generative AI has become embedded in the tools students use to search, write, create, translate, organize, and receive feedback, the boundary between "using AI" and "not using AI" has become increasingly difficult to define. More importantly, treating AI use as a binary choice does little to help students understand the different roles AI can appropriately play in learning.

Microsoft is combining its security information and event management and threat protection products within Microsoft Defender, creating what the company calls an Integrated Security Operations Center built for an era of AI-driven attacks.

#6900. Microsoft Expands AI Security Platform to Address Agentic AI Security Risks

Microsoft is expanding the scope of its AI-powered Project Perception security platform to address the growing concern that AI agents may have access to sensitive information and systems, yet don't always behave as expected.

In a company blog post, Hayete Gallot, executive vice president of Microsoft Security, outlined how Microsoft plans to use Project Perception to monitor, manage, and contain what it calls "agentic insiders." These are AI agents deployed within an environment that can access data, use software tools, and make decisions without constant human supervision. While they can improve productivity, Microsoft warns that they also introduce security risks, even when they haven't been compromised by an attacker.

"Agents are unpredictable," Gallot wrote. "They will adapt as the context changes, may not behave as intended, and will collaborate and collude with other agents to reach their objectives, without needing to be compromised."

Project Perception was first introduced in July. The platform, which entered public preview Aug. 3, is designed to help security teams respond to increasingly sophisticated cyber threats using AI-powered automation.

Instead of leaning only on traditional security alerts and manual investigations, Project Perception uses three kinds of specialized AI agents working together. Red team agents hunt for possible vulnerabilities, blue team agents look into suspicious activity, and green team agents step in to fix problems and strengthen defenses.

To spot threats and respond faster, the system pulls in security data from identities, endpoints, cloud environments, applications, and other sources.

Now Microsoft wants to use those same capabilities on the AI agents already running inside enterprise networks. Project Perception would find those agents, give them identities and permissions, and keep an eye on what they do across different systems. It would also keep assessing the risks that come with their actions.

Microsoft also described a plan for "Guardian Agents," specialized AI agents that supervise other agents and step in when their behavior gets risky. That might mean limiting an agent's permissions or blocking certain actions before they cause harm.

"Monitoring and securing those agents must become continuous," Gallot wrote.

The initiative also reflects concerns raised by Microsoft CEO Satya Nadella in an Oct. 10 essay on AI security.

Nadella argued that enterprises should treat powerful AI models much like potential insider security risks, particularly when those models can access confidential information or perform critical business operations.

"We simply can't outsource responsibility for what intelligence does on our behalf," Nadella wrote.

He called for security controls that operate independently of AI models, including restricted permissions, detailed activity logging, and mechanisms that allow administrators to stop an agent's actions.

"In other words, we need to separate the supply of intelligence from the authority over it," Nadella wrote.

Microsoft admits that watching what AI agents do is only part of the answer. Gallot said that future security tools will also need to show why AI models make the choices they do, not just what actions they end up taking.

For more information, go to the Microsoft blog.

Chris Paoli (@ChrisPaoli5) is the associate editor for Converge360.

#6895. Our 2026 Whole Home Makes Stunning Storage a Self-Care Essential

Our homes aren’t just bursting with beautiful design moments. They are also filled with our stuff. As closets brim with clothes, kitchens fill with appliances, and vanities overflow with products, it's undeniable—our homes are only as beautiful as our storage solutions allow them to be.

Bathroom vanities are getting larger. In fact, according to a 2026 survey by Houzz, 21 percent of bathroom projects are ditching the standard 60-inch vanity for 72-inch models, expanding floor plans and storage potential with them.

Clever storage makes spaces livable, transcending aesthetics to make our homes more functional. After all, what use are gorgeous countertops if their glossy facades are too crowded to appreciate? While storage is more a necessity than an accessory, smart solutions can balance being both practical and pretty.

This year’s Whole Home is a master class in curating stunning interiors, with designs boasting the next big color, pattern, and texture trends. No room is overlooked, especially baths, where Americans are spending more and more time unwinding, recharging, and investing in self-care. Designer Anissa Zajac, founder and principal designer at the Indianapolis-based studio, House Seven Design, brought elevated flair to the evolving space with whimsical wallpaper, mixed-metal hardware, and a his-and-hers vanity boasting expansive counter space, leaving visual breathing room between bold design details and the products that define our daily routines.

In addition to designing vast countertops, Zajac focused on texture, opting to place fluted cabinet doors alongside classic wainscoted drawers to add tactile and visual interest to traditionally flat designs. The fluted faces conceal even more storage space, so you can keep your more intimate, personal backstock, or less frequently used products neatly hidden behind spa-like flair.

So, the next time you feel the urge to declutter, remember that when it comes to storage, we’re talking function and form.

#6893. Survey: Organizations Report Positive ROI on AI Investments, Yet Data Readiness and Oversight Lag Behind

According to a survey by data and AI services provider Further, enterprises are reporting benefits from artificial intelligence even as their data preparation and oversight processes remain incomplete.

The company's recent State of Enterprise AI Maturity research surveyed 515 U.S.-based AI decision-makers. Just 5% believed their organizations' data was largely ready for AI.

The findings suggest that gaining value from individual AI applications and preparing to operate them more broadly are separate challenges. Reported returns alone do not establish that an organization has the foundations to sustain or expand those results.

Further found that 37% of respondents' organizations had paused, scaled back, or abandoned an underperforming AI initiative in the previous 12 months. Among that group, data quality or availability was the most frequently cited main reason, at 24%.

Meanwhile, 29% reported completing a formal data audit and remediation plan. The research also placed 69% of organizations in the exploring, experimenting, or implementing stages of adoption.

Further distinguishes data prepared for conventional reporting from data prepared for AI. Its explanation emphasizes data origins and documentation, along with access controls that support ongoing evaluation and governance.

For readers assessing their own readiness, the practical issue is whether data supports the requirements of a particular application. A collection suitable for a dashboard may need additional preparation before it can reliably support an AI system.

Seventy-seven percent reported a positive return on AI investment. However, only 3% said their organizations had a documented AI-audit process.

The findings measure different things: perceived financial benefit and the existence of a repeatable oversight process. Neither substitutes for the other.

"In reality, confidence without a documented process is exposure," said Kierra Dotson, associate director of AI strategy and governance at Further. "Closing that gap doesn't take new technology; it requires an auditable governance process."

For the full report, visit the Further site (registration required).
